(
\Delta\eta_{\text{jj}}
,
m_{\text{jj}}
, and centrality of
\PH
candidates relative to the VBF jets) and the four-vectors of the two VBF jets. The DNN has three output nodes (
\mathcal{P}_{\Pg\Pg\PH\PH}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{VBF}}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{bkg}}
), proportional to the likelihood of that event corresponding to the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
,
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
, or background classes, respectively. A selection is then applied in order to significantly reduce the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
contamination using a
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
vs.
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
discriminant, defined as
\mathcal{D}_{\Pg\Pg\PH\PH}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{VBF}}/(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{VBF}}+\mathcal{P}_{\Pg\Pg\PH\PH})
. The requirement on
\mathcal{D}_{\Pg\Pg\PH\PH}
retains 80% of the preselected SM
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
signal while rejecting about 90% of the expected SM
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
contribution. After these selections,
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
constitutes about 45% of the total expected signal. Any events not satisfying the
\mathcal{D}_{\Pg\Pg\PH\PH}
requirement are assigned to the inclusive,
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
-dominated, category.

The
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
signal is separated from the remaining background via a “VBF-vs-background” score, defined as
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{VBF}}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{VBF}}/(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{VBF}}+\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{bkg}})
. The
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{VBF}}
discriminant is able to reject the background in the VBF
\mathrm{SR_{4\PQb}}
by two orders of magnitude, while retaining a SM
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
signal efficiency of about 55%. The background prediction is obtained via the same methodology of reweighting of
\mathrm{SR_{2\PQb}}
events, including the VBF category selections described in Section
0.5.2
. In the VBF category, the statistical precision of the reweighted
\mathrm{SR_{2\PQb}}
template is much less than that of the inclusive
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
category and this dominates the total uncertainty in the background prediction. It is, therefore, sufficient to assess the background variance with bin-by-bin uncertainties derived from the spread among
k
-fold background predictions alone, without introducing shape uncertainties as defined in Eq. (
1
), which would have a negligible impact. The background model is validated on
\mathrm{SR_{3\PQb}}
data events including all VBF category selections and is found to model the observed data adequately.

Signal extraction

In the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
category, a monotonic transformation of the SvsB classifier score,
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
, distribution is performed, such that the expected distribution for the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
signal in the
\mathrm{SR_{4\PQb}}
is uniform. The
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
signal is then measured via a binned maximum likelihood fit to the transformed SvsB classifier score distribution in the
\mathrm{SR_{4\PQb}}
, as shown in Fig.
11
for the pre-ParkingHH (left) and post-ParkingHH (right) data. The simple equidistant binning in the transformed SvsB classifier score used in the fit provides a measurement sensitivity compatible with results obtained from more complex binning optimization procedures.

Figure 11:
Post-fit distributions of the SvsB classifier score in the
\mathrm{SR_{4\PQb}}
of the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
resolved analysis for data (black points) and the predicted background (blue filled histograms), for pre-ParkingHH (left) and post-ParkingHH (right) data. The distributions of the SM
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
(orange line) and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
(red line) signals, scaled to improve their visibility, are overlaid. Notations are as in Fig.
8
.

In the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
exclusive category, Fig.
12
shows the distribution of the
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{VBF}}
discriminant in pre-ParkingHH (left) and post-ParkingHH (right) data. Similarly to the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
analysis, a transformation is applied to the
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{VBF}}
distribution such that the predicted
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
distribution is uniform. The number of bins was chosen to maintain a sensitivity within 5% of more aggressive binning options while keeping the total number of bins in the fit as minimal as possible, ensuring that shape uncertainties in the background prediction as defined in Eq. (
1
) would be negligible.

Figure 12:
Post-fit distributions of the SvsB output score in the
\mathrm{SR_{4\PQb}}
of the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
resolved analysis reported for data (black points) and the predicted background (blue filled histograms) for pre-ParkingHH (left) and post-ParkingHH (right) data. The distributions of the SM
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
(orange line) and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
(red line) signals, scaled to improve their visibility, are overlaid. Notations are as in Fig.
8
.

0.5.3
The second approach: GNN-based classification including
\PZ\PZ/\PZ\PH
regions

Another approach to the search for
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
in the resolved topology is presented below. An important difference with the approach described in Section
0.5.2
is in the treatment of the SRs and CRs, which are modified to target the
\PH\PH
,
\PZ\PZ
, and
\PZ\PH
processes, following the strategy in Ref.
[
77
]
. Due to their larger cross sections, the SM
\PZ\PZ
and
\PZ\PH
processes are expected to be observed in the 4
\PQb
final state before the
\PH\PH
process. Since they share the same experimental challenges as
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
, it is important to pursue them in tandem as they serve as valuable validation tools for all analysis methodologies, and in particular for the background estimation procedure. Another key difference is that at the core of this approach is a custom graph-based neural network architecture, referred to as
FeynNet
, used for the derivation and validation of the background model, for process classification, and for signal-vs-background discrimination.

Events with at least four AK4 jets with
{\pt>70}
, 50, 40, and 35
\GeV
in pre-ParkingHH and 30
\GeV
in post-ParkingHH data,
{\abs{\eta}<2.5}
, and satisfying tight identification criteria are selected. Events are further required to have at least two
\PQb
-tagged jets using the
pnet
medium WP, and the average
\PQb
tagging score of the leading two is required to satisfy the online selection. Additional jets with
{\pt>30\GeV}
and
{\abs{\eta}<4.7}
, not
\PQb
tagged, are considered as VBF candidate jets.

The analysis uses four mutually exclusive regions, defined by the number of jets satisfying the loose, medium, and tight
pnet
WPs, and optimized for this approach. These regions include 2
\PQb
events; events in which the three leading in
\PQb
tagging score jets satisfy the tight WP while the fourth one can be either medium (3T1M) or loosely (3T1L)
\PQb
tagged; and events in which the three leading in
\PQb
tagging score jets satisfy the medium but failing the tight WP (3MnT). For all of these regions, the four jets leading in
\PQb
tagging score are considered as the boson candidate jets, and the pairing is performed with the method described in Section
0.5.1
. Once the two boson candidates are paired, four overlapping regions in the plane defined by the reconstructed dijet masses of the leading (
m_{\PH_{1}}
) and subleading (
m_{\PH_{2}}
) candidates in boson
\pt
, are used to target the
\PH\PH
,
\PZ\PZ
, and
\PZ\PH
signals in the 4
\PQb
final state. Defining the variables
X_{\PH\PZ}
,
X_{\PZ\PH}
, and
X_{\PZ\PZ}
as:

X_{\PH\PZ}=\sqrt{\left[\frac{m_{\PH_{1}}-125\GeV}{\sigma_{m_{\PH_{1}}}}\right]^{2}+\left[\frac{m_{\PH_{2}}-89\GeV}{\sigma_{m_{\PH_{2}}}}\right]^{2}}

(2)

X_{\PZ\PH}=\sqrt{\left[\frac{m_{\PH_{1}}-91\GeV}{\sigma_{m_{\PH_{1}}}}\right]^{2}+\left[\frac{m_{\PH_{2}}-120\GeV}{\sigma_{m_{\PH_{2}}}}\right]^{2}}

(3)

X_{\PZ\PZ}=\sqrt{\left[\frac{m_{\PH_{1}}-91\GeV}{\sigma_{m_{\PH_{1}}}}\right]^{2}+\left[\frac{m_{\PH_{2}}-89\GeV}{\sigma_{m_{\PH_{2}}}}\right]^{2}},

(4)

the SRs take the form:

\text{SR:}\ (R_{\PH\PH}<30)\ \cup\ (X_{\PZ\PZ}<2.6)\ \cup\ (X_{\PZ\PH}<1.9)\ \cup\ (X_{\PH\PZ}<1.9).

(5)

The centroids in
m_{\PH_{2}}
in Eqs. (
2
) and (
4
) are chosen to be 89
\GeV
to reflect a residual
\pt
dependence in the reconstructed mass after the application of the flavor-inclusive JES corrections described in Section
0.4.3
. The denominator in the
\PZ\PZ
,
\PZ\PH
, and
\PH\PZ
terms corresponds to the approximate mass resolution of the reconstructed boson candidates, as estimated from simulation to be about 10% of the reconstructed dijet mass. The CRs surround the SRs, with their outer bounds defined as:

\text{CR:}\ (52<m_{\PH_{1}}<180\GeV)\ \cup\ (50<m_{\PH_{2}}<173\GeV).

The signals are extracted in two regions,
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
and
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1L}}
, where the inclusion of
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1L}}
improves the results by approximately 6%. The boundaries of the SR and CR in the 3T1M region are shown in Fig.
13
for the total
\PH\PH
,
\PZ\PZ
, and
\PZ\PH
yield as taken from simulation and normalized to the expected yield (left) and the observed data (right) as a function of
m_{\PH_{1}}
and
m_{\PH_{2}}
.

Figure 13:
The expected
\PH\PH
,
\PZ\PH
, and
\PZ\PZ
signal yields as estimated from simulation (left) and the observed data (right) for the Run 3 data set, in the 3T1M region, as a function of the reconstructed masses of the leading and subleading in
\pt
\PH
candidates. The signal region is defined by the union of the regions enclosed by the dashed red lines.

The
FeynNet
algorithm structures the four-vectors of the four leading in
\PQb
tagging score jets (
j
) as well as the two leading in
\pt
VBF candidates (
f
) with opposite-
\eta
, if present, as a collection of nodes in a graph. Subsequently, it constructs all unique combinations of dijets and builds composite nodes for higher-level structures. These nodes represent the possible decay topologies of the
\PH\PH
-quadjet and VBF-dijet (
ff
) systems. The input features used include: the four-vectors of all six jets considered, the minimum distance between an
\PH
and a VBF candidate jet (
\min\Delta R_{jf}
), the distances between each
\PH
candidate jet (
\Delta R_{jj}
) and VBF candidate jets (
\Delta R_{ff}
), the
\PH
-dijet candidates (
\Delta R_{\PH\PH}
), and the
\PH\PH
-quadjet and VBF-dijet system (
\Delta R_{\PH\PH,ff}
). In the case of the boson candidate jets, a flag is provided as an additional input feature to denote the pairing assignment. The
FeynNet
algorithm returns a set of event classification probabilities derived from the linear combination of the different objects.

Three sets of trainings of the
FeynNet
algorithm are performed. The first one is used to estimate the total background in the SRs using CRs in data. The second training is used to discriminate the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
,
\PZ\PH
, and
\PZ\PZ
processes from the derived background model and its output scores construct the variables used in the final signal extraction. The third training is used to validate the background estimation procedure in a signal-depleted region.

To estimate the background in the
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
(
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1L}}
), a
FeynNet
classifier is trained using data in CRs to model the kinematic differences between them. For the
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
, the classifier is trained with data from the most populated CR,
\text{CR}_{2\PQb}
, as well as from
\text{CR}_{\text{3T1M}}
. For the
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1L}}
, the training uses data from the
\text{CR}_{\text{3MnT}}
and
\text{CR}_{\text{3T1L}}
. Based on the classifier’s output probability scores, weights, defined as
w_{\text{3T1M}}=\mathcal{P}(\text{CR}_{\text{3T1M}})/\mathcal{P}(\text{CR}_{2\PQb})
and
w_{\text{3T1L}}=\mathcal{P}(\text{CR}_{\text{3T1L}})/\mathcal{P}(\text{CR}_{\text{3MnT}})
, are applied to the data in the signal-depleted
\text{SR}_{2\PQb}
and
\text{SR}_{\text{3MnT}}
, respectively. The reweighted
\text{SR}_{2\PQb}
and
\text{SR}_{\text{3MnT}}
data provide the background estimate for the
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
and
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1L}}
, respectively. For both cases, a
k
-fold ensembling method is applied with
k=30
, where 75 (25)% of the data events in the CRs are used for the training (evaluation). The choice of
k
ensures that the estimated variance has converged and remains stable as the number of trained models increases.

A second
FeynNet
model is trained as a multiclassifier in the
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
to discriminate the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
,
\PZ\PH
, and
\PZ\PZ
processes from the background model derived in the previous step. Three nonoverlapping regions targeting
\PH\PH
,
\PZ\PZ
, and
\PZ\PH
production, respectively, are defined according to which of the output signal probabilities is the largest. Figure
14
shows the distribution of the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
,
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
,
\PZ\PZ
, and
\PZ\PH
signal processes in the plane defined by the three probability scores. About 85% and 93% of the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
signal events, respectively, are correctly assigned to the
\PH\PH
region. Small contaminations of the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
(
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
) events exist in the
\PZ\PZ
and
\PZ\PH
regions, with 5% and 10% (3% and 4%), respectively.

Figure 14:
Distribution of the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
,
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
,
\PZ\PZ
, and
\PZ\PH
signal processes, normalized to unity, as a function of the three
FeynNet
probability scores.

The
\PH\PH
region is further divided into
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
regions based on the presence of two VBF candidate jets in the event. To improve the separation of
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
events in the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
region, a selection is applied on the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
-vs-
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
score, built from the probabilities:

D(\text{$\Pg\Pg\PH\PH$-vs-$\PQq\PQq\PH\PH$ })=\frac{\mathcal{P}(\Pg\Pg\PH\PH)}{\mathcal{P}(\Pg\Pg\PH\PH)+\mathcal{P}(\PQq\PQq\PH\PH)},

which returns about 92 (27)% of the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
(
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
) events in the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
region and keeps about 8 (73)% of the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
(
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
) events in the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
region. For each category, an SvsB score is defined as:

D(\text{signal-vs-bkg})=\frac{\mathcal{P}(\text{signal})}{\mathcal{P}(\text{signal})+\mathcal{P}(\text{bkg})},

(6)

and used in the final signal extraction.

The background estimation procedure is validated with a third
FeynNet
training in
\text{SR}_{\text{3MnT}}
. The 3MnT data set is randomly split into 10 subsets and the background estimation procedure is repeated in each of them. The kinematic weights, derived from a training in
\text{CR}_{2\PQb}
and
\text{CR}_{\text{3MnT}}
and defined as
w_{\text{3MnT}}=\mathcal{P}(\text{CR}_{\text{3MnT}})/\mathcal{P}(\text{CR}_{2\PQb})
, are applied on the observed data in
\text{SR}_{2\PQb}
to estimate the background in the
\text{SR}_{\text{3MnT}}
. The distributions of the
\mathcal{D}\text{($\Pg\Pg\PH\PH$-vs-bkg)}
,
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PQq\PQq\PH\PH$-vs-bkg)}
,
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PZ\PZ$-vs-bkg)}
, and
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PZ\PH$-vs-bkg)}
scores are shown in Fig.
15
for the average of the 10 background models and the observed data in the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
,
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
,
\PZ\PZ
, and
\PZ\PH

\text{SR}_{\text{3MnT}}
, respectively. A selection on the SvsB scores above 0.05 is applied in the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
,
\PZ\PZ
, and
\PZ\PH
SRs. This selection is found to improve the agreement between the background model and data in the
\text{SR}_{\text{3MnT}}
and reduce the systematic uncertainties related to the background estimation without affecting the
\PH\PH
sensitivity in the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH

\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
and
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1L}}
. Due to the limited number of events in the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH

\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
, no such selection is applied.

Figure 15:
Post-fit distribution of the
\mathcal{D}\text{($\Pg\Pg\PH\PH$-vs-bkg)}
(upper left),
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PQq\PQq\PH\PH$-vs-bkg)}
(upper right),
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PZ\PZ$-vs-bkg)}
(lower left), and
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PZ\PH$-vs-bkg)}
(lower right) scores in the validation region
\text{SR}_{\text{3MnT}}
for data (black points) and the predicted background (gray filled histograms) with the Run 3 data set. Notations are as in Fig.
8
.

Systematic uncertainties in the background model arise, first, from the finite size of data sample in the CRs used to train the
FeynNet
classifier, and second, from the extrapolation to the SR from a different region of phase space. The variance of the background models in
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
and
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1L}}
is estimated by comparing the average of the background models to each of the thirty models trained, as a function of the discriminant scores, defined in Eq. (
6
). The differences among the models are parameterized with a set of three orthogonal Fourier basis functions, following the procedure used in Ref.
[
77
]
, resulting in three uncorrelated shape uncertainties. The same method is repeated separately for the
\mathcal{D}\text{($\Pg\Pg\PH\PH$-vs-bkg)}
,
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PZ\PZ$-vs-bkg)}
, and
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PZ\PH$-vs-bkg)}
scores, and for the
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
and
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1L}}
. Due to the limited number of events in the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH

\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
, an uncorrelated uncertainty is derived from the root-mean-square of all background model variations. Any residual difference between the average model and the variations is included as an additional uncorrelated uncertainty (up to 2%). The extrapolation uncertainty is assessed in the
\text{SR}_{\text{3MnT}}
, by fitting the observed data in the
\text{SR}_{\text{3MnT}}
with the average of the 10 background models. The post-fit pulls of each of the three basis-function coefficients give an estimate of the bias of each component. The magnitude of the pulls is used to assign an additional systematic uncertainty for each of the basis-function coefficients.

The transformed distributions of the
\mathcal{D}\text{($\Pg\Pg\PH\PH$-vs-bkg)}
and
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PQq\PQq\PH\PH$-vs-bkg)}
scores used in the maximum likelihood fit to extract the
\PH\PH
signal are shown in Fig.
16
for the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH

\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
,
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH

\text{SR}_{\text{3T1L}}
, and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH

\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
categories.

Figure 16:
Post-fit distributions of the transformed
\mathcal{D}\text{($\Pg\Pg\PH\PH$-vs-bkg)}
score in the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH

\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
(left) and
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH

\text{SR}_{\text{3T1L}}
(middle) categories, and
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PQq\PQq\PH\PH$-vs-bkg)}
score in the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH

\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
(right) category for data (black points) and the predicted background (gray filled histograms) for the Run 3 data set. The distributions of the SM
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
(orange line) and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
(red line) signal processes, scaled to improve their visibility, are also overlaid. Notations are as in Fig.
8
.

Validation with
\PZ\PZ
and
\PZ\PH
processes

A crucial test before extracting the
\PH\PH
signal is the validation of the analysis strategy using the SM
\PZ\PZ
and
\PZ\PH
processes. A search for the SM
\PZ\PZ
and
\PZ\PH
processes is performed with a binned maximum likelihood fit on the
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PZ\PZ$-vs-bkg)}
and
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PZ\PH$-vs-bkg)}
discriminant scores in the exclusive
\PZ\PZ
and
\PZ\PH

\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
and
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1L}}
regions, respectively. Figure
17
shows the
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PZ\PZ$-vs-bkg)}
(left) and
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PZ\PH$-vs-bkg)}
(right) discriminant scores, and compares the observed data to the predicted background, with the
\PZ\PZ
and
\PZ\PH
signals overlaid in each case, in the
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
(upper row) and
\text{SR}_{\text{3T1L}}
(lower row).

Figure 17:
Post-fit distribution of the transformed
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PZ\PZ$-vs-bkg)}
score in the
\PZ\PZ

\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
(upper left) and
\PZ\PZ

\text{SR}_{\text{3T1L}}
(upper right) categories, and
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PZ\PH$-vs-bkg)}
score in the
\PZ\PH

\text{SR}_{\text{3T1M}}
(lower left) and
\PZ\PH

\text{SR}_{\text{3T1L}}
(lower right) SR for data (black points) and the predicted background (gray filled histograms) for the Run 3 data set. The distributions of the SM
\PZ\PZ
(green line) and
\PZ\PH
(purple line) processes are also overlaid. Notations are as in Fig.
8
.

The observed
\PZ\PZ
and
\PZ\PH
signal strengths are reported in Table
0.5.3
, in terms of the signal strength modifiers,
\mu_{\PZ\PZ}
and
\mu_{\PZ\PH}
, defined as
{(\sigma\mathcal{B})_{\text{obs}}/(\sigma\mathcal{B})_{\text{SM}}}
, where
\sigma
is the signal production cross section and
\mathcal{B}
is the branching fraction. Upper limits at 95%
\CL
on the signal strengths are also set, assuming no signal exists. Systematic uncertainties, described in Section
0.5.5
, affecting the signal models and the background prediction are incorporated in the fit via nuisance parameters. Results are determined following the CMS statistical procedures, which are described in detail in Section
0.5.6
.

The measured signal strength for the
\PZ\PZ
process is
{\mu_{\PZ\PZ}=3.5^{+2.0}_{-1.8}}
, which is compatible with the SM expectation at the level of 2 standard deviations. For the
\PZ\PH
process, the measured signal strength is
{\mu_{\PZ\PH}=0.2^{+1.6}_{-1.5}}
. Compared to previous results reported in Ref.
[
77
]
using the LHC Run 2 data, a similar sensitivity is achieved with almost half the integrated luminosity. This sensitivity gain can be attributed to a variety of improvements in the Run 3 methods, as described in Section
0.4
, and validates the relative improvements obtained for the
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
limits shown in Section
0.5.6
. Expanding the analysis SR from the
\PH\PH
-sensitive SR (
{R_{\PH\PH}<30\GeV}
) to the SR defined in Eq. (
5
), allows for a simultaneous search for the
\PH\PH
,
\PZ\PZ
, and
\PZ\PH
processes with a small effect on the
\PH\PH
sensitivity (about 2% loss). The combinations of the Run 2
[
77
]
and Run 3 data sets in the
\PZ\PZ
and
\PZ\PH
categories are summarized in Table
0.5.3
and yield observed (expected) upper limits at 95%
\CL
of 4.2 (2.6) for
\mu_{\PZ\PZ}
and 3.2 (2.1) for
\mu_{\PZ\PH}
.

\topcaption

The observed and expected upper limits at 95%
\CL
on the
\mu_{\PZ\PZ}
and
\mu_{\PZ\PH}
, observed signal strength and significance for the Run 2
[
77
]
and Run 3 data sets and their combination. The upper limits on
\mu_{\PZ\PZ}
(
\mu_{\PZ\PH}
) are obtained from a fit on the
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PZ\PZ$-vs-bkg)}
(
\mathcal{D}\text{($\PZ\PH$-vs-bkg)}
) score under the hypothesis of no
\PZ\PZ
(
\PZ\PH
) signal, while the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
,
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
and
\PZ\PH
(
\PZ\PZ
) processes are fixed to their SM values. The uncertainties given correspond to the 68%
\CL
intervals.

0.5.4
Improved analysis with Run 2 data

This analysis targets the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
process in the resolved topology, and extends the search for
\PZ\PZ\to 4\PQb
and
\PZ\PH\to 4\PQb
using Run 2 data, described in Ref.
[
77
]
.

Events are initially selected using the Run 2 triggers sensitive to three
\PQb
-tagged jets, identified with the
DeepCSV
algorithm
[
45
]
. The offline selection further requires at least four AK4 jets with
{\pt>40\GeV}
and
{\abs{\eta}<2.4}
. The
DeepJet
algorithm identifies the four jets with the highest
\PQb
tagging scores, designating them as the
\PH\PH
candidates. A subsequent pairing scheme is optimized to reconstruct
\PZ\PZ
,
\PZ\PH
, and
\PH\PH
events in the
m_{\PH_{1}}
–
m_{\PH_{2}}
plane. To maximize the analysis sensitivity, the
\PQb
tagging WP used to define the 3
\PQb
and 4
\PQb
regions is optimized based on the expected limits on
\PH\PH
production.

A key difference from the approaches described in Sections
0.5.2
and
0.5.3
, as well as the previous result with Run 2 data
[
60
]
, lies in the background estimation strategy. In this approach, the
\ttbar
contribution is taken from simulation, while the dominant QCD multijet background is derived from data. The measurement of the QCD multijet background is performed using events in the 3
\PQb
region, defined to be exclusive of the events in the 4
\PQb
region. Due to the tighter
\PQb
tagging requirements of the Run 2 triggers, the 2
\PQb
region is inaccessible. To enhance the statistical power of the data in the 3
\PQb
region, the offline
DeepJet
WP is relaxed to a WP corresponding to a 70–80% efficiency, as measured in simulated events with
\PQb
jets. These 3
\PQb
events are then weighted using two factors derived from CRs in data to predict the QCD multijet background in the 4
\PQb
region. The first weight accounts for the differences in jet and
\PQb
jet multiplicities between the two samples. It is calculated by parameterizing a functional form based on the number of jets and
\PQb
jets in a signal-depleted region of the
m_{\PH_{1}}
–
m_{\PH_{2}}
plane, as described in Ref.
[
77
]
. The second weight addresses residual kinematic differences during the extrapolation from the 3
\PQb
to the 4
\PQb
region.
Both weights are derived from a multivariate classifier based on the hierarchical combinatorial residual (HCR) architecture introduced in Ref.
[
77
]
, which is built on a series of convolutional neural networks. The HCR classifier is designed to separate the QCD multijet and
\ttbar
components in data events with three and four
\PQb
-tagged jets. The input features of the network are the four-momenta of the jets, subject to the aforementioned kinematic requirements.

The same HCR architecture is also trained to distinguish signal from background. Five classes are considered, each yielding an output probability score for the QCD multijet,
\ttbar
,
\PZ\PZ
,
\PZ\PH
, and
\PH\PH
processes. The training leverages simulations for all processes, except for the QCD multijet process, which is estimated from data. Mutually exclusive signal extraction regions are defined according to which of the
\PH\PH
,
\PZ\PH
, and
\PZ\PZ
output probabilities is the largest. The sum of the three aforementioned probabilities for events where the
\PH\PH
probability is largest,
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
, is monotonically transformed such that the expected
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
signal distribution in the
\PH\PH

\mathrm{SR_{4\PQb}}
is uniform. The
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
signal is then measured via a maximum-likelihood fit to the transformed
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
distribution. Figure
18
shows the transformed
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
in the
\PH\PH

\mathrm{SR_{4\PQb}}
for the observed data, the QCD multijet background estimate from CRs in data, the
\ttbar
background from simulation, and the expected
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
signal.

Figure 18:
The fitted signal+background distribution of the signal probability,
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
, in the
\PH\PH

\mathrm{SR_{4\PQb}}
. The black points show the 4
\PQb
events from data. The yellow and blue regions show the predictions from the QCD multijet model and the
\ttbar
simulation, respectively. The prediction for the SM
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
signal distribution is given by the red histogram, multiplied by 100. The lower panel shows the data-to-background ratio, with the hatched area representing the background uncertainty.

Systematic uncertainties in the QCD multijet prediction are evaluated utilizing 3
\PQb
data events to construct a synthetic data set. This data set is specifically designed to have the same statistical power as the 3
\PQb
data events, which notably exceeds that of the 4
\PQb
events by a factor of fifteen. Comprehensive details regarding the construction and characteristics of this data set are provided in Ref
[
77
]
. To ensure a robust assessment of these uncertainties, the QCD multijet estimation procedure is iteratively applied to fifteen distinct subsamples from this synthetic data set. The resulting differences in the signal-vs-background distributions among these fifteen QCD multijet predictions and their overall average are then parameterized by a set of orthogonal Fourier basis functions, thereby allowing for the systematic uncertainties to be assigned to the nominal background model.

0.5.5
Systematic uncertainties

Systematic uncertainties can affect both the predicted number of signal events and the predicted shape of the SvsB classifier score used for the
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
signal extraction. Theoretical uncertainties arise from the limited precision in the computation of the inclusive and differential cross sections of the simulated
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
processes, and their effect is fully correlated between event categories and data-taking periods. Experimental uncertainties comprise those (i) related to the imperfect simulation of the detector response, (ii) in the integrated luminosity measurement, and (iii) in the modeling of the pileup interactions. All systematic uncertainties are modeled in the signal extraction fit as nuisance parameters with log-normal or Gaussian external constraints, as described in Section
0.5.6
.

Theoretical uncertainties affecting the inclusive SM
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
cross section include the choice of the PDF set (
\pm 1.5\%
), variation in the QCD
\mu_{\text{R}}
and
\mu_{\text{F}}
scales (
+2.1\%,-4.9\%
), the value of
\alpS
(
\pm 1.7\%
), and the uncertainty related to the value and definition of the top quark mass (
+4\%,-18\%
). The values of these uncertainties are taken from Refs.
[
101
,
20
]
. The theoretical uncertainty in the
\PH\to\PQb\PAQb
branching fraction is 2.5%
[
116
]
. Uncertainties in the inclusive
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
production cross section from QCD scale variations (
+0.05\%,-0.03\%
), PDF and
\alpha_{S}
(
\pm 2.7\%
) are taken from Refs.
[
118
,
95
,
94
]
. The dependence on
\kappa_{\lambda}
of the inclusive cross section and event kinematic features for both
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
production, and related uncertainties, is accounted for when interpreting the results of this analysis for non-SM hypotheses. Theoretical uncertainties in the predicted
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
signal acceptance in the SR are determined from simulated events. These include the uncertainty arising from the limited knowledge of
NNPDF
parameters, evaluated following Ref.
[
34
]
, variations in the
\mu_{\text{R}}
and
\mu_{\text{F}}
scales by a factor 2 or 0.5, and variations of initial- and final-state radiation scales of the parton shower.

Experimental uncertainties with the largest impact impact on the signal prediction are those related to the JES and JER, which originate from various sources with limited correlations
[
41
]
. These uncertainties depend on the jet kinematics (
\pt
,
\eta
) and flavor composition. Variations of the jet four-momenta caused by shifts in the JES and JER have an impact on both the number of events accepted in the SR and the shape of the SvsB classifier score distribution. As described in Section
0.4.3
, the JES and JER closure of the post-regression jet
\pt
was assessed on
\PZ\to\PGm\PGm+\PQb
data and residual differences between data and simulation in the post-regression JES (1–2%) and JER (4–8%) are included as systematic uncertainties, correlated across event categories but uncorrelated among data-taking periods. Uncertainties in the
\PQb
tagging efficiency also have an important impact on the
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
signal prediction and, as for JES and JER, originate from a number of independent sources
[
74
,
73
]
. The uncertainty sources with the largest impact are the systematic uncertainty components in the measurement of the
\PQb
jet tagging efficiency and the light-flavor quark or gluon jet misidentification rate. Uncertainties in the measured simulation-to-data trigger efficiency SFs, in the integrated luminosity, and associated with the modeling of pileup interactions are also considered but have a small impact on the result.

The background prediction is fully based on CRs in data, in both the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
categories and the normalization of the total background yield is fit to data, with no prior constraint, independently for pre-ParkingHH and post-ParkingHH data. The background prediction in each bin of the SvsB classifier score has an uncertainty related to the limited count of the
\mathrm{SR_{2\PQb}}
data events used in the background reweighting procedure. Random fluctuations in the background model prediction are accounted for via independent binwise uncertainties, which consider both the statistical uncertainty in the
\mathrm{SR_{2\PQb}}
data and the variance among individual
k
-fold background predictions. These uncertainties are treated as uncorrelated across the bins of the SvsB score distributions and are modeled using the Barlow–Beeston “lite“ technique
[
84
,
26
]
. In the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
category, shape uncertainties affecting the background prediction as a function of the SvsB score distributions are included using a set of independent Fourier basis functions, independently for pre-ParkingHH and post-ParkingHH data. These account for the statistical variance and potential extrapolation bias in the background prediction. The methods used to quantify and account for these shape uncertainties in the background prediction are described in detail in Sections
0.5.2
and
0.5.3
for the first and second approaches, respectively. The shape uncertainties are at the percent-level at low and intermediate SvsB classifier score, increasing to as much as 5–6% in the highest-score bins. Because of the limited number of data events in the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
category and the coarse binning used, the binwise uncertainty is sufficient to account for any variance or bias effects.

0.5.6
Results

The
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
signal, from both the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
production modes, is extracted via a binned maximum likelihood fit to the output of the SvsB classifier in the SRs. The distributions in data collected in the different data-taking periods are fitted simultaneously. The signal strength modifiers
\mu_{\Pg\Pg\PH\PH}
and
\mu_{\PQq\PQq\PH\PH}
for the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
production modes, respectively, are defined as the ratio between the measured
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
rate and the expectation from the SM, while
\mu_{\PH\PH}
similarly corresponds to the total
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
signal rate including both production modes.
Confidence intervals on the signal strengths are estimated using a profile likelihood ratio test statistic
[
85
]
, in which systematic uncertainties are modeled as nuisance parameters.
Upper limits at 95%
\CL
on the corresponding production cross sections are derived using the modified frequentist
\CLs
criterion
[
7
]
with an asymptotic approximation
[
112
,
124
]
. Results are determined using the CMS statistical analysis tool
Combine

[
78
]
, which is based on the
RooFit

[
128
]
and
RooStats

[
119
]
frameworks. The results presented in this paper for
\mu_{\PQq\PQq\PH\PH}
are determined without any constraint on
\mu_{\Pg\Pg\PH\PH}
, which is profiled in the relevant fits.

Table
0.5.6
summarizes the expected 95%
\CL
upper limits and best fit signal strengths on
\mu_{\Pg\Pg\PH\PH}
,
\mu_{\PQq\PQq\PH\PH}
, and
\mu_{\PH\PH}
from the two approaches described in Sections
0.5.2
and
0.5.3
. The first approach achieves stronger expected exclusion values compared to the second approach for the signal strength modifiers, as well as for anomalous values of
\kappa_{\lambda}
and
\kappa_{\text{2V}}
. Table
0.5.6
summarizes the measured signal strength modifiers and the corresponding 68%
\CL
intervals, as well as the observed 95%
\CL
upper limits for each signal strength modifier. No significant excess is observed over the background-only expectation, and the measured signal rates are well compatible between the two approaches.

\mu_{\PH\PH}

0.7
. This gives further confidence in the reliability and stability of the uncertainties employed in the background model prediction.

The sensitivity of this measurement is substantially improved compared to the previously published result based on Run 2 data
[
60
]
. Scaled to the same integrated luminosity, the improvement in expected limit is more than a factor 2. This major step forward in the CMS
\PH\PH
measurement program implies that, as of today, the
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
resolved analysis is the most sensitive individual analysis in probing the
\PH
self-coupling for a given integrated luminosity. The large improvement arises from a combination of key factors: a fully redesigned trigger strategy, innovative new methods in
\PQb
jet tagging and
\pt
regression, and several new analysis techniques.

Updated results with Run 2 data

The
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
signal is extracted via a binned maximum likelihood fit to the SvsB distribution in the
\PH\PH

\mathrm{SR_{4\PQb}}
. Systematic uncertainties are treated as nuisance parameters with either Gaussian (shape uncertainties) or log-normal (normalization uncertainties) function priors included in the likelihood function. The observed (expected) signal strength
\mu_{\Pg\Pg\PH\PH}
is found to be
3.1^{+2.4}_{-1.9}
(
1.0^{+2.1}_{-1.9}
). The 95%
\CL
observed and expected upper limits on the signal strength are set to be
10.0
and
5.9^{+3.8}_{-1.9}
, respectively. Compared to the previous measurement, the updated result achieves about a 25% improvement in the expected upper limit. The analysis furthermore benefits from an improved background modeling and a more robust treatment of associated uncertainties. Constraints are also set on the
\kappa_{\lambda}
and the observed (expected) allowed intervals outside of which the theoretical prediction for
\PH\PH
production is excluded at 95%
\CL
are
[-7.7,16.2]
(
[-3.8,11.0]
). These new results, summarized in Table
0.5.6
, supersede the prior published results on the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
signal strength reported in Ref.
[
60
]
.

0.6
The merged topology

0.6.1
Overview

The merged category targets
\PH\PH
events in which both Higgs bosons are highly Lorentz boosted. In this regime, the
\PQb
quarks from each
\PH
decay are emitted in a collimated configuration and their hadronization products can be reconstructed as a single AK8 jet. For
\PH
with
{\pt\gtrsim 300\GeV}
, this approach is more effective than using a pair of AK4 jets. Although the QCD multijet background remains substantial, its differential cross section falls more steeply with increasing event energy compared to the
\PH\PH
signal, improving signal-vs-background discrimination at high
\pt
. As a result, despite representing a small fraction of the inclusive
\PH\PH
signal, merged topologies offer enhanced sensitivity to rare processes, as demonstrated in the previous Run 2 search
[
66
]
.

The Run 3 data set is analyzed in the merged topology by leveraging the dedicated trigger paths and improved
\bbbar
-identification introduced during Run 3. Events passing the trigger requirements must contain at least two AK8 jets with
{\pt>300}
and 250
\GeV
, respectively and with a soft-drop mass
{m_{\text{SD}}>50\GeV}
for each jet. The two AK8 jets with the highest and second-highest
\bbbar
-tagging scores, according to the
GloParT
algorithm described in Section
0.4.4
, are selected as the primary (
\PH_{1}
) and secondary (
\PH_{2}
)
\PH
candidates, respectively. The leading jet is also required to have a
\bbbar
-tagging score exceeding the threshold applied at the trigger level.

In this region, the dominant background arises from QCD multijet events, particularly those with one or more AK8 jets containing
{\Pg\to\bbbar}
, which can mimic the
\PH\to\PQb\PAQb
signature. This process constitutes about 75% of the total background in the preselected region. The
\ttbar
process contributes most of the remaining background, about 24%. The selected AK8 jets in
\ttbar
events originate from a variety of sources, such as partially merged hadronic top quark decays, merged hadronic
\PW
boson decays,
{\Pg\to\bbbar}
, or the fragmentation of single
\PQb
quarks. The remaining 1% of the background arises from
\PV+\text{jets}
,
\PV\PV
, and
\PV\PH
processes. The primary observables to reject these backgrounds are the
GloParT
regressed masses and
\bbbar
tagging scores of the two
\PH
candidate AK8 jets.

The merged topology is particularly sensitive to
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
production for certain non-SM coupling hypotheses
{\kappa_{\text{2V}}\neq 1}
that significantly increase the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
cross section, especially for highly Lorentz-boosted
\PH
pairs
[
93
,
92
,
30
,
6
]
. To enhance the sensitivity to the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
production mode, events are further categorized using additional AK4 jets that do not overlap with the AK8 jets. These jets are required to have
{\pt>25\GeV}
and
{\abs{\eta}<4.7}
. Additional selections on the dijet system (jj), specifically
m_{\text{jj}}
and
\Delta\eta_{\text{jj}}
, are also applied either explicitly or through a multivariate algorithm. All preselected events not assigned to the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
category are considered in the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
categories.

The merged analysis is performed following two complementary approaches, both of which use ML to separate the signal from background via an SvsB classifier. The first approach introduces new methods for the merged analysis in both the background estimation and the signal extraction, employing a background prediction method similar to that used in the resolved analysis (Section
0.5.2
) and training the SvsB classifier with reweighted data sideband events. The second approach models the dominant QCD multijet background via a parametric extrapolation from a highly populated data sideband and uses MC simulated events to train the SvsB classifier, following the strategy adopted in the previous analysis of Run 2 data
[
70
]
.

0.6.2
The first approach: background estimation using multidimensional DNN-based reweighting

In this section, we present a new approach to the measurement of
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
in the merged topology. At its core is a background estimation strategy derived from the methods used in the resolved analysis described in Section
0.5
. This approach eliminates any dependence on simulated QCD multijet background events or the use of parametric functions for the background model, enabling a more performant and more flexible signal extraction strategy than was possible with previous methods. In addition to the selections mentioned in Section
0.6.1
, the regressed masses of both AK8 jet
\PH\to\PQb\PAQb
candidates, as estimated by the
GloParT
algorithm, are required to be between 50 and 200
\GeV
. The
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score for both jets is also required to exceed a value of 0.65. In the SR, the
m_{\text{reg}}({\PH_{1}})
is additionally required to be between 100 and 150
\GeV
. The expected signal fraction after applying these selections is less than 0.1%.

In order to better isolate the SM
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
process, a feedforward DNN with four hidden layers was trained to separate the
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
signal from the backgrounds based on: a) the
\pt
and
\eta
of the two
\PH
candidates, b) the
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score and
m_{\text{reg}}
of the
\PH\to\PQb\PAQb
candidate jet with highest
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score (
\PH_{1}
), c) the kinematics (
\pt
,
\eta
,
m_{\PH\PH}
) of the
\PH\PH
system, and d) the
\Delta R
and mass of each
\PH
candidate and the nearest nonoverlapping AK4 jet. The dominant features used by the DNN to suppress the QCD multijet background are the
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{1})
and
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{1})
, while
\ttbar
is further suppressed by using variables that aim to reconstruct the full set of top quark decay products by combining the
\PH\to\PQb\PAQb
candidate AK8 jet with nearby AK4 jets. The
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score is discretized according to the calibration regions described in Section
0.4.4
.

The prediction for the dominant QCD multijet background in the SvsB DNN training is obtained by reweighting data events in a
\bbbar
tag score sideband region, as described later in this section. The SvsB DNN achieves an additional background rejection of more than one order of magnitude while preserving a signal efficiency of about 60%. Further background rejection is achieved by tightening the
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})
selections. Events in the preselected region are partitioned into three categories based on the SvsB DNN classifier score, denoted as
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
, and the value of
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})
:

•

High-purity SR (HPSR):
{\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}>0.9}
and
{T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})>0.95}
;

•

Low-purity SR (LPSR):
{\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}>0.9}
and
{0.85<T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})<0.95}
;

•

Validation region (VR):
{\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}>0.7}
and
{T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})>0.7}
. Veto events selected in either the HPSR or LPSR.

The HPSR region corresponds to about 90% of the measurement sensitivity in the merged analysis, while the LPSR and VR regions serve to validate the background model.

The background model employs a DNN to perform a multidimensional reweighting of data events, learning from events in a mass sideband (
{50<m_{\text{reg}}({\PH_{1}})<100\GeV}
) the mapping of observables from a region,
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,A}}
, with a loosened
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})
requirement
{0.05<T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})<0.65}
, to another a region,
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,B}}
, with the same
{T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})>0.65}
selection as applied in the SR. The DNN output is then used to apply a weight to data events with
{100<m_{\text{reg}}({\PH_{1}})<150\GeV}
and
{0.05<T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})<0.65}
, denoted
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,C}}
, to obtain the background prediction in the SR. The reweighting DNN was trained from input features in common with the SvsB DNN described above. A schematic of the regions used in this background estimation technique is shown in Fig.
19
(left). The requirement of
{T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})>0.05}
is applied in all regions to ensure that the AK8 jets selected in background events have properties similar to those of the
\PH\to\PQb\PAQb
signal, minimizing the extrapolation of the background model.

Figure 19:
Left: schematic diagram showing the regions used for the background estimation strategy used in the merged channel. The purity in QCD multijet events is reported in each of the background-enriched regions (
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,A}}
,
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,B}}
, and
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,C}}
). Right: schematic diagram of the signal (orange area) and CRs (QCD in green,
\ttbar
in azure) in the plane defined by the
m_{\text{reg}}({\PH_{1}})
and
m_{\text{reg}}({\PH_{2}})
.

The background model is derived from an ensemble of
k=20
reweighting DNNs, following exactly the same methodology as described for the resolved channel in Section
0.5.2
. For each of the trainings, 75% of the data from the
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,A}}
and
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,B}}
regions are randomly selected for training and the initial weights of the DNN are also randomly sampled. The background prediction is then derived by applying the median per-event weight among the
k
-fold background predictions to
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,C}}
data events. The spread, defined in Section
0.6.4
, in the background predictions among the different
k
-folds is assigned as a systematic uncertainty in the background prediction for each bin of the fitted observable. The subdominant
\ttbar
background constitutes up to 20% of the total background in some regions. In order to explicitly separate the
\ttbar
contribution in data, the reweighting DNN is trained to differentiate simulated
\ttbar
events from data events in
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,A}}
and
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,B}}
. The data events in
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,C}}
are then assigned a weight of

\mathrm{weight}=\frac{\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{CR_{data,B}})-\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{CR_{\ttbar,B}})}{\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{CR_{data,A}})-\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{CR_{\ttbar,A}})},

(7)

where
\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{CR_{data,B}})
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{CR_{data,A}})
estimate the likelihood that an event corresponds to data in
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,B}}
or
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,A}}
, respectively, while
\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{CR_{\ttbar,B}})
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{CR_{\ttbar,A}})
estimate the likelihood that an event derives from the
\ttbar
process in a given region. This procedure effectively performs a multidimensional subtraction of the expected
\ttbar
contribution in simulation from the background prediction based on CRs in data. The total background prediction in the SR is then obtained from the sum of the reweighted
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,C}}
data events and the simulated
\ttbar
prediction in the SR. This procedure makes it possible to differentiate the predicted QCD multijet and
\ttbar
background contributions and to explicitly constrain the normalization of each process separately in the fit.

The normalization of the
\ttbar
prediction in each SR category (HPSR, LPSR, and VR) is constrained in situ by data in a
\ttbar
-enriched CR (HP-TT-CR, LP-TT-CR, and TT-VR), defined with exactly the same selections applied as in the SR except that the
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{1})
and
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
are required to be between 150 and 200
\GeV
, as shown in Fig.
19
(right). This procedure reduces the dependence of the analysis on the
\ttbar
background simulation. The SR category definitions include selections on
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
, the output score of the SvsB DNN, which is trained using
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{1})
as an input feature. With the aim of aligning as much as possible the characteristics of the selected events in the
\ttbar
CRs to those of
\ttbar
events in the corresponding SR categories, the
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
score is evaluated in the
\ttbar
CRs with the
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{1})
value shifted to match the range in the SR,
{100<m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{1})<150\GeV}
. The
\ttbar
process is the dominant contribution in these regions and is expected to be roughly 80% of the total background in the HP-TT-CR and LP-TT-CR regions. The event yields in these
\ttbar
-enriched regions are included in the signal extraction fit such that the
\ttbar
normalization in each SR category (HPSR, LPSR, VR) is fit individually to data without any prior constraint. This effectively removes any dependence on the modeling of the
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
or
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score distributions in
\ttbar
simulation. As shown in Fig.
19
(left), a low-mass QCD CR is also defined per SR category with all the selections of the corresponding SR category applied except for a shift in the
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{1})
requirement,
50<m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{1})<100\GeV
, following similar logic to that employed for the
\ttbar
CRs. The event yield in each QCD CR is included in the signal extraction fit to improve the constraint on the normalization of the QCD multijet prediction.

The new background estimation method employed in the merged analysis, based on a reweighting of
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,C}}
data events (as shown in Fig.
19
right), yields a background prediction for multiple observables, providing flexibility in the signal extraction. Two complementary signal extraction methods are presented, each based on a fit to a different observable. The “mass fit” method performs a fit to the
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
distribution in each SR category, as was done in previous searches in this channel
[
66
]
, while the “DNN fit” method extracts the signal via a fit to the output of an SvsB classifier DNN,
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
, following an approach similar to that used in the resolved analysis described in Section
0.5
. With the DNN fit method, the SR categories are redefined with respect to those described earlier, still partitioning events based on the same
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})
criteria but without any
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
selections applied so that the full
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
distribution can be included in the signal extraction fit.

The mass fit method has the advantage of fitting a physical observable in which the expected
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
signal as well as some of the background processes (
\PV\PH
,
\PV\PV
) manifest as resonances in the
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
distribution. The primary systematic uncertainty following the mass fit method is the limited constraint achievable for the QCD multijet background normalization from CRs in data. The DNN fit method, on the other hand, has the advantage of including more data events in the fit and therefore better constraining the QCD multijet background predictions. It also enables the SvsB classifier DNN to leverage the full event information, including correlations between the
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
and the other observables. The dominant systematic uncertainties with the mass fit and DNN fit methods differ significantly–by pursuing both methods and obtaining consistent results, we build further confidence in the procedures used throughout.

The mass fit method

In the mass fit method, the signal is extracted via a binned maximum likelihood fit to the
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
distribution in each category, in addition to the event yields in the
\ttbar
- and QCD-enriched CRs. Figure
20
shows these distributions in the HPSR (left), LPSR (center), and VR (right) categories. The overlaid signal contribution is the a priori prediction scaled by a multiplicative factor to enhance its visibility. A relatively coarse bin width, about twice as wide as the expected mass resolution, was chosen for the HPSR so that each bin in the
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,C}}
sideband data distribution used to derive the background prediction contains at least 10 events. This binning configuration provides a comparable sensitivity, within about 5%, to finer binning choices, while also minimizing the statistical variance in the background prediction. In the LPSR and VR regions, which have many more events, more granular bins were chosen–this has a minimal impact on the merged analysis sensitivity, but enables a more stringent test of the background model.

Figure 20:
Pre-fit and post-fit distributions of the fitted observables for the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
-inclusive HPSR (left), LPSR (center), and VR (right) categories of the merged analysis following the mass fit method. Distributions are shown for data (black points) and the different background contributions from QCD multijet,
\ttbar
,
\PV+\text{jets}
,
\PV\PV
, and
\PZ\PH
productions. The expected distributions for SM
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
(orange) and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
(red) signals are overlaid and scaled by a multiplicative factor to improve their visibility. Notations are as in Fig.
8
.

In both the HPSR and LPSR, the spread among the
k
-fold background predictions in most bins of the
m_{\PH_{2}}
distribution, including those where the expected signal lies, is comparable to the per-bin statistical uncertainty in the reweighted
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,C}}
data events (5–20%). The
k
-fold variance, included in the bin-by-bin uncertainties, increases substantially, up to 40–50%, for the rightmost bin(s) with
{m_{\PH_{2}}>160\GeV}
. In this region, the
\ttbar
relative contribution to the total background is larger and the increased spread in the
k
-fold predictions reflects a larger uncertainty in the per-event
\ttbar
subtraction performed by the reweighting (Eq. (
7
)). The background prediction models the observed data well in all regions including the VR, which contains an order of magnitude more data events than the LPSR and therefore validates the background model with significantly higher precision than relied upon in the fit to the HPSR.

The DNN fit method

The SvsB DNN developed for the DNN fit method has the same architecture and input features as used for the SvsB DNN employed in the mass fit analysis, with the addition of
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
as an input feature. The SR categories are redefined with respect to those used for the mass fit method, removing any selections on
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
. Figure
21
shows the
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
distributions in the HPSR (left), LPSR (center), and VR (right) categories of the DNN fit analysis. A minimal coarse binning was chosen for the HPSR in order to simplify the fit and to ensure that any variance in the background model correlated across bins is negligible. In the LPSR, instead, a more granular binning was chosen in order to more precisely test the background model. The variance in the background model prediction, evaluated from the spread among
k
-fold background predictions, is 1% or less in the background-dominated bins and increases to 10–20% in the rightmost high-score bins where the expected signal purity is highest. The observed data in the VR, which has an order of magnitude more events than the LPSR, are consistent with the background prediction, within uncertainties.

Figure 21:
Pre-fit and post-fit distributions of the fitted observables for the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
-inclusive HPSR (left), LPSR (center), and VR (right) categories of the merged analysis following the DNN fit method. Distributions are shown for data (black points) and the different background contributions from QCD multijet,
\ttbar
,
\PV+\text{jets}
,
\PV\PV
, and
\PZ\PH
productions. The expected distributions for SM
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
(orange) and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
(red) signals are overlaid and scaled by a multiplicative factor to improve their visibility. Notations are as in Fig.
8
.

The VBF
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
category

Similar to the resolved
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
analysis described in Section
0.5.2
, events with at least two additional AK4 jets with
{\pt>25\GeV}
and
{\abs{\eta}<4.7}
are considered in the merged
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
category if the maximum
m_{\text{jj}}
among additional jet pairs exceeds 400
\GeV
and their
\eta
separation satisfies
{\abs{\Delta\eta_{\text{jj}}}>3.5}
. Since measurements of
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
at high
\pt
are particularly sensitive to deviations in
\kappa_{\text{2V}}
, the merged analysis specifically targets sensitivity to an anomalous
\kappa_{\text{2V}}=0

\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
signal, a representative example of the non-SM
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
signals that could be present in this search.

In the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
merged analysis, the same background model for the QCD multijet background was employed, using the same region definitions reported in Fig.
19
. A feedforward SvsB DNN classifier was trained using the same input features used in the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
merged analysis described in Section
0.5.2
, as well as: a) the AK4 dijet invariant mass
m_{\text{jj}}
and
\abs{\Delta\eta_{\text{jj}}}
, b) the four-vectors of the two VBF jets, c) the centrality of each
\PH
candidate. The DNN aims to separate the
\kappa_{\text{2V}}=0

\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
signal from the QCD multijet and
\ttbar
backgrounds. All events in the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
merged analysis are required to pass the same minimum threshold on the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
category SvsB DNN output score,
\mathcal{P}_{\text{VBFHH}}>0.9
. This requirement has an efficiency of about 86% for the
\kappa_{\text{2V}}=0

\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
signal and reduces the background by nearly two orders of magnitude after the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
category selections described above. All events that fail this selection are included in the merged analysis
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
production category. Following a similar methodology to that used in the inclusive
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
category, the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
merged analysis SR was then partitioned based on the
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}({\PH_{2}})
into three regions with different levels of signal purity–VBF-HPSR, VBF-LPSR, and VBF-VR.

Figure 22:
Pre-fit and post-fit distributions of the fitted observables for the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
HPSR (left) and LPSR (right) categories of the merged analysis. Distributions are shown for data (black points) and the different background contributions from QCD multijet,
\ttbar
,
\PV+\text{jets}
,
\PV\PV
, and
\PZ\PH
productions. The expected distributions for SM
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
(orange) and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
(red) signals are overlaid and scaled by a multiplicative factor to improve their visibility. Notations are as in Fig.
8
.

The signal in the VBF merged analysis is extracted via a fit to the
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
distribution in each category, including the event yield in dedicated QCD- and
\ttbar
-enriched CRs per category, as depicted in Fig.
19
(right). The measurement uncertainty in the VBF category is dominated by statistical uncertainties, so the fit is simplified and the mass fit method is used. Figure
22
shows the fitted distributions in the VBF-HPSR (left) and VBF-LPSR (right) categories. The number of events in the VBF SR categories is very small, with approximately one event expected in the VBF-HPSR. The binning of the
m_{\text{reg}}({\PH_{2}})
distribution in the fit is therefore very coarse. The sensitivity is driven by the middle bin with
m_{\text{reg}}({\PH_{2}})
between 100 and 150
\GeV
, while the other bins effectively serve for further background model validation. The variance in the background model, evaluated from the spread in
k
-fold background predictions, ranges from 10–15% for
{m_{\text{reg}}({\PH_{2}})<150\GeV}
and from 30–60% for
{m_{\text{reg}}({\PH_{2}})>150\GeV}
, where the
\ttbar
contribution is the most significant.

0.6.3
The second approach: background estimation using a parametric alphabet method

A different approach is explored in the merged topology following the strategy adopted in the analysis of Run 2 data
[
70
]
. In this approach, a boosted decision tree (BDT) is trained to discriminate the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
process from the QCD multijet and
\ttbar
backgrounds. The input features of the BDT are similar to the ones used in the approach described in Section
0.6.2
and include kinematic properties of the
\PH
and VBF jet candidates, the
\PH\PH
system, the
N
-subjettiness (
\tau_{32}
)
[
126
]
of the two
\PH
candidates, the
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score of the
\PH
candidate with the leading
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score, angular separation between the final state objects and event-level observables. The
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score and the regressed mass of the subleading in
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}

\PH
candidate jet,
m_{\text{reg}}({\PH_{2}})
, are excluded from the input features as these variables are used to define the event categories of the signal extraction.
The
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score is discretized following the working points calibrated for the
GloParT
algorithm. The BDT yields four output probabilities:
\mathcal{P}(\Pg\Pg\PH\PH)
,
\mathcal{P}(\PQq\PQq\PH\PH)
,
\mathcal{P}(\text{QCD multijet})
and
\mathcal{P}(\ttbar)
, which are combined to define two discriminant scores targeting the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
categories:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\text{$\Pg\Pg\PH\PH$-vs-bkg})

\displaystyle=\frac{\hfil\mathcal{P}(\Pg\Pg\PH\PH)}{\mathcal{P}(\Pg\Pg\PH\PH)+\mathcal{P}(\text{QCD})+\mathcal{P}(\ttbar)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\text{$\PQq\PQq\PH\PH$-vs-bkg})

\displaystyle=\frac{\hfil\mathcal{P}(\PQq\PQq\PH\PH)}{\mathcal{P}(\PQq\PQq\PH\PH)+\mathcal{P}(\text{QCD})+\mathcal{P}(\ttbar)}.

Four nonoverlapping SRs are constructed based on a two-dimensional (2D) selection applied on the
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})
score and the respective
\mathcal{D}(\text{$\Pg\Pg\PH\PH$-vs-bkg})
and
\mathcal{D}(\text{$\PQq\PQq\PH\PH$-vs-bkg})
scores, as illustrated in Fig.
23
. Three of these SRs are sensitive to the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
production (
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
SR 1–3) and one is sensitive to the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
production (
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
SR). The highest signal purity SR,
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
SR 1, is defined by requiring events to satisfy tight WPs for both
\mathcal{D}(\text{$\Pg\Pg\PH\PH$-vs-bkg})
and
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})
. Events not in the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
SR 1 are assigned to the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
SR only if they satisfy a tight
D(\text{$\PQq\PQq\PH\PH$-vs-bkg})
WP and a loose
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})
WP. Remaining events are grouped into
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
SR 2 and 3, defined by successively lower selections on the
\mathcal{D}(\text{$\Pg\Pg\PH\PH$-vs-bkg})
and
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})
WPs, as shown in Fig.
23
. The categorization selections are chosen to maximize the sensitivity to the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
process in the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
SRs and to the
\kappa_{\text{2V}}=0

\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
process in the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
SR. The events failing the SR conditions, but satisfy a very loose selection on
\mathcal{D}(\text{$\Pg\Pg\PH\PH$-vs-bkg})
and
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}(\PH_{2})
, are enriched in QCD multijet events and used for the background modeling (QCD CR).

Figure 23:
Schematic diagrams showing the SRs and QCD CR (gray) used in the merged analysis described in Section
0.6.3
. Left: the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
SR 1 (red), 2 (blue), and 3 (orange) are defined based on successively lower selections on the
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score of the
\PH_{2}
candidate and the
\mathcal{D}(\text{$\Pg\Pg\PH\PH$-vs-bkg})
score. Right: the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
SR (purple) is defined based on a tight selection on
\mathcal{D}(\text{$\PQq\PQq\PH\PH$-vs-bkg})
and a loose selection on
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
of the
\PH_{2}
candidate.

The two dominant backgrounds after the SR selection are the QCD multijet process and the
\ttbar
process. The QCD multijet background is estimated from data in the QCD CR using the parametric alphabet method, following the strategy employed in the Run 2 analysis in the merged topology
[
70
]
, as well as previous searches
[
54
,
46
,
48
,
43
]
. The estimation is performed as a function of
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
, after subtracting
\ttbar
(8%) and other minor contributions (2%). In the absence of correlations between
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
and the
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score or BDT discriminator, the QCD multijet background is expected to have the same
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
distribution in the QCD CR as in the SRs. Therefore, since the
GloParT

\bbbar
tagger is trained to be independent of the
\PH
mass assumption and the BDT inputs are chosen to avoid correlations with
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
, the QCD CR can be used to predict the shape of the QCD multijet background in the SRs. The background model is defined as the product of the
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
shape obtained from the QCD CR and an
n^{\text{th}}
-order Bernstein polynomial transfer function, with the parameters determined from the fit, to account for any residual correlations between the
m_{\text{reg}}
and
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score or BDT discriminator. A Fisher F-test and a goodness-of-fit test are performed in the QCD CR and each SR to determine the order of the polynomial. A zero-order polynomial is found to be sufficient in all four SRs. The overall normalization is considered as a systematic uncertainty and a floating normalization is assigned and determined from the sidebands.

The
\ttbar
background is estimated using simulated events with corrections extracted in dedicated CRs in data, which will be described in the following paragraphs. While the overall kinematic distributions are generally well modeled, several features relevant to this analysis require these corrections. The
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score is calibrated using a CR enriched in
\ttbar
events including one semileptonic top quark decay. This region is defined using single-lepton triggers and requires exactly one isolated lepton with
{\pt>50\GeV}
,
{\ptmiss>50\GeV}
, and one high-
\pt
AK8 jet with
{\pt>300}
(250)
\GeV
for 2022 (2023) and
{m_{\text{SD}}>50\GeV}
. Events with additional leptons or AK8 jets are rejected. A
\PQb
-tagged AK4 jet, well separated from the lepton and the jet, is required to enhance
\ttbar
purity. Finally, a requirement on
{\tau_{32}<0.5}
is applied on the AK8 jet. The correction factors are found to be in the range of 10–20% in the highest
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score region. Simulation-to-data correction factors are also derived as a function of
\tau_{32}
after subtracting the estimated contributions from non-
\ttbar
processes.

The recoil of the
\ttbar
system is corrected using a CR enriched in fully hadronic
\ttbar
events. Events are selected by requiring two large-radius jets, each with
{\pt>450\GeV}
,
{\abs{\eta}<2.5}
,
{m_{\text{SD}}>50\GeV}
,
{T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}>0.1}
, and
{\tau_{32}<0.46}
. Correction factors are derived by comparing the distribution of the
\pt
of the
\ttbar
system in data and simulation in bins of dijet
\pt
(
\pt(\mathrm{jj})
), with typical corrections of 1–2% across most of the recoil momentum.

The distribution of the BDT discriminant is validated in the fully hadronic
\ttbar
CR for both the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
SRs. Events are required to have two large-radius jets with
{\pt>300\GeV}
,
{m_{\text{SD}}>40\GeV}
, and
{T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}>0.1}
. The
\PH_{1}
candidate jet must have
{150<m_{\text{reg}}<200\GeV}
and
{\tau_{32}<0.6}
, while the
\PH_{2}
candidate jet must have
{60<m_{\text{reg}}<250\GeV}
. The ratio between background subtracted data and
\ttbar
simulation prediction is taken as the correction factor as a function of
\mathcal{D}(\text{$\Pg\Pg\PH\PH$-vs-bkg})
for the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
SR 1–3, or
\mathcal{D}(\text{$\PQq\PQq\PH\PH$-vs-bkg})
for the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
SR. In the highest BDT score bins, corrections of approximately 20–50% are derived to account for discrepancies between data and simulation.

Figure 24:
The background-only fit distributions of the regressed mass of the subleading
\PH
candidate
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
in
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
SR 1 (upper left),
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
SR (upper right),
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
SR 2 (lower left), and
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
SR 3 (lower right). The SM
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
signal are overlaid in the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
SRs scaled by different factors. The targeted
\kappa_{\text{2V}}=0

\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
signal is also overlaid in the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
SR. Notations are as in Fig.
8
.

Figure
24
shows the
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
distributions for all categories. Signal is extracted via a fit to these distributions. The
\ttbar
background, as well as the minor backgrounds from
\PV+\text{jets}
,
\PV\PV
, and single
\PH
production, are obtained from the MC templates with corrections based on CRs in data.

0.6.4
Systematic uncertainties

Many of the theoretical and experimental uncertainties affecting either the
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
signal prediction or that of background processes modeled via simulation are estimated following the same procedures as outlined for the resolved analysis in Section
0.5.5
. Uncertainties that are exclusive to the merged analysis are those affecting the
\bbbar
tagging efficiency, the jet mass scale (JMS) and resolution (JMR) for the two
\PH\to\PQb\PAQb
-candidate jets, and the uncertainties in the QCD multijet and
\ttbar
background predictions.

The simulated predictions for the
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
signal,
\PZ\PH
, and
\PV\PV
processes are corrected with the
\bbbar
tagging efficiency SFs reported in Section
0.4.4
. The corresponding uncertainties, which range 10–20%, are included in the fit as independent nuisance parameters for each WP (VHP, HP, and MP) and data-taking period. These uncertainties are uncorrelated between AK8 jets with
{\pt>450\GeV}
, for which efficiency corrections are measured using high-
\pt
\PZ\to\PQb\PAQb
decays, and AK8 jets with
{\pt<450\GeV}
, for which no explicit calibration was made. The
\bbbar
tagging efficiency for jets with
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
score less than the range of the MP, which contribute only to the regions with small signal purity, is assigned a relative uncertainty twice as large than the closest calibrated WP. Differences in the JMS and JMR between data and simulation, measured with
\PZ\to\PQb\PAQb
decays for the same
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
WPs, are used to correct predictions from the simulation, and the corresponding uncertainties are propagated as systematic uncertainties in the simulated predictions.

The systematic uncertainties with the largest impact in the merged analysis are those affecting the QCD multijet background prediction, followed by uncertainties in the
\ttbar
background prediction.
In the first approach, the QCD multijet background prediction is derived from a reweighting of data events in a
\bbbar
tag sideband region (
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,C}}
). Because of the close synergies with the background prediction method used in the resolved analysis, the uncertainty in the QCD multijet prediction is evaluated following a very similar procedure to that described in Section
0.5.5
. The normalization of the QCD multijet prediction is fit to data with no prior constraint, independently per SR category (HPSR, LPSR, and VR). The spread, defined as the half-difference between 84% and 16% quantiles, among each of the
k=20
individual background predictions is added in quadrature to the binwise statistical uncertainty from the
\mathrm{CR_{QCD,C}}
data. These uncertainties are uncorrelated across bins of the fitted distributions. The normalization of the
\ttbar
background prediction is fit to data without any prior, independently per SR category, and binwise uncertainties take into account the limited statistical precision of the
\ttbar
simulation.

For the second approach, the uncertainty in the QCD multijet background prediction is propagated through profiling of the unconstrained transfer factor parameters in the signal extraction fit.
For the
\ttbar
background, we propagate uncertainties in the corrections for
T_{\text{X}\PQb\PAQb}
,
\tau_{32}
, and the recoil
\pt(\text{jj})
distribution. These systematic uncertainties are implemented as lognormal constraints because the variations from the uncertainties are not dependent on
m_{\text{reg}}(\PH_{2})
. The bin-by-bin statistical uncertainty in the correction on the
\mathcal{D}(\text{$\Pg\Pg\PH\PH$-vs-bkg})
and
\mathcal{D}(\text{$\PQq\PQq\PH\PH$-vs-bkg})
shapes for
\ttbar
, derived in the all-hadronic
\ttbar
CR, is propagated through additional nuisance parameters to the signal extraction fit.

0.7
Combined results

The analyses of the resolved and merged topologies are combined, using the approach with the best expected sensitivity for each topology. This corresponds to the resolved analysis “first approach” described in Section
0.5.2
and the merged analysis “first approach” described in Section
0.6.2
using the mass-fit method. Although the high-
\pt
events selected in the merged analysis are a small fraction of the events accepted in the resolved analysis, the resolved analysis SvsB classifier learns that events with high-
\pt
jets have significantly better S/B and therefore tends to assign high
\mathcal{P}_{\text{HH}}
values to these events. It is therefore important to address this event overlap between topologies, making sure that no events are considered twice and ensuring that the combination of analyses yields the best possible expected sensitivity.

Of all the event categories considered, the merged analysis HPSR category achieves the highest expected S/B. It is therefore natural to assign priority to merged HPSR events, removing them from the resolved analysis before performing a combined signal extraction fit including both analyses. In the combined fit, the merged HPSR categories are included following the mass fit method (Section
0.6.2
), as this minimizes the scale of the event overlap with the resolved analysis and because the mass-fit method achieves the most stringent expected constraints on
\kappa_{\lambda}
. This removes 2–10% of the expected signal events from the resolved analysis at high SvsB classifier score and has a very small effect on the background, removing a few events from the resolved
\mathrm{SR_{4\PQb}}
and affecting the background distribution by much less than 1%. The overlap removal degrades by about 5% the expected 95%
\CL
upper limit on
\mu_{\PH\PH}
from the resolved analysis for all
\kappa_{\lambda}
hypotheses, compared to the results summarized in Section
0.5.6
. Omitting the VR and LPSR regions from the merged analysis degrades the merged analysis-only expected limit by about 14%. Alternative combination configurations were tested, such as the inclusion of the merged analysis LPSR regions or using the merged analysis DNN fit method (Section
0.6.2
) rather than the mass fit. In all cases, the combination approach described above achieved comparable to or better sensitivity than the alternative options.

Systematic uncertainties affecting the
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
signal prediction from theoretical sources are fully correlated across the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
resolved and merged analyses, as well as across data-taking periods. Experimental uncertainties in the signal prediction from pileup, integrated luminosity, JES, and JER are correlated among all event categories but uncorrelated across data-taking periods. The remaining experimental uncertainties, such as the trigger efficiency, the
\PQb
tagging efficiencies, the
\bbbar
tagging efficiency, and the AK8 jet mass scale and resolution are uncorrelated across event categories and data-taking periods. The uncertainties in the QCD multijet background predictions of the two analyses, affecting either the rate or the shape of the backgrounds, are also uncorrelated. The dominant systematic uncertainties impacting the combined fit are the uncertainty in the normalization of the QCD multijet background in the merged HPSR as well as the shape and normalization uncertainties in the background prediction of the resolved analysis. The remaining uncertainties have a more marginal impact on the combined result, with the exception of the uncertainty in the theoretical prediction for the inclusive
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
cross section related to the scheme and scale choice of the virtual top mass
m_{\text{top}}
, which has a significant relative impact for
\mu_{\PH\PH}
values near the exclusion limits.

The observed data in the combined result are compatible with the best fit signal-plus-background prediction, with a
p
-value of 72% from a goodness-of-fit test. Figure
25
(left) shows 95%
\CL
upper limits on the
\PH\PH
signal strength
\mu_{\PH\PH}
, assuming that relative contributions of the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
and
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
production modes are fixed to the SM prediction within their uncertainties. Upper limits are presented separately for the resolved
\PH\PH\to 4\PQb
analysis, after overlap removal, for the merged HPSR mass fit category, and for their combination. Upper limits are obtained by fixing the signal event kinematic features to those predicted for the SM
\PH
couplings,
\kappa_{\lambda}=\kappa_{\text{2V}}=1
. The observed (expected) upper limit on
\mu_{\PH\PH}
from the combined fit is 4.38 (4.39). Figure
25
(right) shows, instead, the 95%
\CL
upper limits on the
\PQq\PQq\PH\PH
signal strength,
\mu_{\PQq\PQq\PH\PH}
, calculated with the
\Pg\Pg\PH\PH
signal strength profiled.

upper limits on the signal strength of
\PH\PH
production from the resolved analysis with overlap removed, the merged HPSR mass fit category, and their combination. The orange and blue bands represent, respectively, the 68 and 95%
\CL
intervals around the expected limit. Right: the same breakdown of 95%
